Last week I had this guy who was convinced his wife was cheating with their landscaper, which is such a cliché, right? Who even has a dedicated landscaper in this economy? \n\nBut anyway, during the intake with him, I just couldn't stay in my chair. I kept popping up, pacing, looking out the blinds. And it's not just work. It happens during my tutoring sessions, and even at my Thursday book club. We're reading this historical fiction thing right now set in 1920s Paris, which I usually love, but... well, people are really starting to notice that I can't just sit and listen to the discussion. I'm always jumping up to refill a glass or look at a bookshelf or whatever, and they're starting to give me these looks.\n\nDoctor: When you're in these situations where you can't stay still, do you also find it hard to concentrate or focus on what's being said, or do you find your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I actually hear everything they're saying. My mind isn't really wandering off, I just... I just have to be moving while I listen. \n\nLike with the book club, I know exactly what's going on in the story. It's about this American expat who gets tangled up with the Hemingway crowd, which, honestly, I think is a little overdone? Like, everyone wants to write about Hemingway's Paris. I went to Paris once, actually, right after I finished my bachelor's. We stayed in this tiny little place in Montmartre, and the stairs were so steep I thought I was going to break my neck every time I went down to the cafe on the corner. The coffee over there is amazing, though. So rich. I tried buying a French press when I got back to the States to recreate it, but it's just never the same, you know? It always comes out kind of gritty. I don't know if I'm using the wrong grind for the beans or what, but I eventually just gave up and went back to my drip machine. \n\nBut... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, concentrating. No, I'm fully tuned in to the conversation or the client, I just physically cannot stay glued to the chair.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your mind isn't wandering, but I'm curious—when you're talking or thinking, do your thoughts ever feel like they're moving really fast, or do you find yourself jumping from one topic to another quickly?\n\nPatient: Fast thoughts? Jumping from topic to topic? Um, I don't really think so. I mean, my thoughts just kind of flow normally, I think. I just like to talk about things as they come to mind. Like, if I'm talking about a case, I might mention the client's car, which reminds me of my first car, this beat-up old Honda Civic. I loved that car. It was this awful mustard yellow color, but it ran forever. I actually sold it to my cousin when I upgraded, and he ended up driving it across the country to California. He lives in San Diego now, which is gorgeous. I went out there to visit him a few years ago and we went to the zoo. Have you ever been? It's massive. We spent the whole day walking around, and I still don't think we saw everything. The pandas were definitely the highlight, though. They're just so round and fluffy. I bought a little stuffed panda in the gift shop for my niece, but honestly, I kind of wanted to keep it for myself. \n\nSo, yeah, I don't think my thoughts are moving super fast or anything. I just have a lot of different things to say, you know?\n\nDoctor: When you feel that need to get up and move around, does it feel like you have a lot of physical energy or restlessness building up inside you that you need to release, or does it feel more like anxiety or nervousness that makes you want to move?\n\nPatient: Anxiety? No, it's not anxiety. I'm not really a nervous person. It's more like... like a motor running inside me, I guess? Like this pressure that just builds up and I have to move to let it out. It's totally physical. \n\nIt kind of reminds me of my golden retriever, Buster. He's such a good boy, but if I don't take him for a long walk in the morning, he just vibrates with energy all day. He'll pace around the living room, bring me every toy he owns, just whining until we go out. We usually walk down by the river. There's this great trail there that used to be an old railway line. They paved it over a few years ago, which is nice for walking, but honestly, I kind of miss the dirt path. It felt more natural, you know? Plus, the pavement gets so hot in the summer, I always worry about Buster's paws. I actually bought him these little dog booties once to protect his feet, but he hated them so much. He did this hilarious high-stepping walk like a dressage horse for about two minutes before he just lay down and refused to move until I took them off. I ended up giving them to my neighbor who has a poodle. Poodles seem more like the bootie-wearing type, anyway. \n\nBut yeah, I guess that's what it feels like. Just this physical need to burn off energy, not nervousness or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this need to move around and this physical restlessness? Has this been going on since you were younger, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, how long? Well, I mean, I've always been an active person, but this specific... like, this really intense need to move? It's been noticeably worse for about eight months. Yeah, right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. \n\nI loved that place. It was a third-floor walk-up in Lincoln Park, right by the conservatory. The light in the living room was just incredible in the afternoons. I had this huge monstera plant that absolutely thrived there. It grew so big I actually had to repot it three times in the five years I lived there. Repotting is such a messy job, though. I always ended up with soil all over the hardwood floors, and then I'd have to sweep it up, but you can never get all the grit, you know? It always feels like you're walking on sandpaper for days afterward. I tried putting down newspaper once, but the water leaked through and stained the wood, which was a whole other disaster. I ended up having to buy this special rug just to cover the spot before the landlord saw it. It was a really nice rug, actually, sort of a Persian style, deep reds and blues. I found it at this antique market on a Sunday morning. The guy wanted way too much for it, but I managed to haggle him down. I'm pretty good at haggling, actually. It's a useful skill in my line of work. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, the moving thing. It's really just been the last eight months or so that it's gotten this bad.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been an active person. When you were a child or teenager, did teachers or family ever say you were hyperactive, or did you have trouble sitting still in school or at the dinner table—even back then?\n\nPatient: School? Oh, yeah. I mean, my grade-school report cards always said I was bright, but they always had these notes about how I couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, just investigating every corner instead of sitting at my desk. \n\nWhich is kind of funny, considering what I do for a living now. But back then, I just really hated those little wooden desks with the chairs attached. They were so restrictive. And they always had those weird grooves at the top for pencils, but my pencils would always roll right off anyway. I used to use those Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the green metal band near the eraser. They were the best. The lead didn't break every five seconds like the cheap ones did. I actually still use them for my fieldwork notes. There's just something about writing in pencil that feels more... permanent, ironically? Like, ink can smear if it rains while you're on a stakeout, but pencil holds up. I got caught in a downpour once outside a warehouse in Gary, Indiana, and my whole notebook was soaked, but I could still read every single word I'd written. Gary's a tough town, though. The wind off Lake Michigan just cuts right through you in the winter. You really have to layer up if you're going to be sitting in a car for hours. Thermal socks are an absolute must. \n\nBut yeah, my mom always said I couldn't sit still when I was a kid. It's just who I am, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been formally diagnosed with ADHD or evaluated for it by a doctor? And if so, were you prescribed any medication for it?\n\nPatient: Diagnosed? No, no one's ever formally evaluated me or anything. Definitely no medication. I mean, my mom just thought I had a lot of energy, you know? She never took me to a doctor for it. \n\nHonestly, I've always been a little wary of doctors anyway. Not you, of course, but just... the whole medical establishment. It started when I broke my arm in high school. I was climbing this massive oak tree in our backyard—I used to love climbing trees, you could see the whole neighborhood from up there. Anyway, a branch snapped, and I fell right onto my left arm. The doctor at the ER set the bone wrong, and they had to re-break it two weeks later to fix it. It was excruciating. Ever since then, I just try to avoid hospitals if I can help it. I don't even like the smell of them. That weird, sterile, rubbing-alcohol smell? It instantly puts me on edge. It reminds me of this one case I worked a few years ago involving medical supply fraud. I had to spend three weeks doing surveillance on this warehouse that distributed rubbing alcohol and bandages. The smell seeped into my clothes, my car, everything. I had to get my car detailed twice just to get the scent out of the upholstery. The guy who did the detailing was great, though. His name was Hector, and he used this citrus spray that smelled amazing. I actually asked him what brand it was, but he said it was a family secret recipe. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no official diagnosis or pills or anything like that. Just me and my energy.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home alone, just relaxing or doing something you enjoy—like working on a case or reading—do you still feel that same need to get up and move around, or does it mostly happen when you're with other people or in situations where you're supposed to stay still?\n\nPatient: When I'm at home alone? Yeah, it's... it's still there. Even with things I actually enjoy doing. Like, I used to love quiet things to just unwind. Yoga, for instance. Or going to my book club. But lately, those quiet things that used to help me relax? They just turn into me whispering comments to the person next to me, or rustling my papers, or finding some excuse to get up and move around instead of just being still. \n\nI was actually at book club last Tuesday. We were reading this mystery novel—which, honestly, the detective work in it was laughable. The protagonist didn't even check the suspect's alibi against the cell tower data. I mean, that's day-one stuff! If I ran my investigations like that, I'd have lost my license years ago. You always check the digital footprint. People lie, but their phones don't. I had this one case where a guy swore he was in Milwaukee for a conference, but his phone pinged off a tower three blocks from his wife's sister's house in Evanston. Turns out he was hiding his car in her garage. It was a classic setup. The garage was one of those old detached ones, with the peeling white paint and the heavy wooden doors that scrape the concrete when you open them. I had to sit in my car across the alley for six hours waiting for him to come out. I drank so much coffee that day, I thought my heart was going to beat right out of my chest. It was from this little diner on the corner, they made it way too strong. \n\nBut yeah, even when I'm trying to relax and read or do yoga, I just... I can't stay still anymore. I'm always shifting or finding a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on your investigation cases or other tasks that require sustained focus, do you find it hard to stick with them from start to finish, or do you generally complete what you start even though you need to move around while doing it?\n\nPatient: Focusing on cases? Um... I don't really know if I have trouble sticking with them. I mean, I guess I get them done eventually? I'm not really sure if it's hard for me to finish things or if I just do them my own way. \n\nI do have to move around a lot while I'm working, though. Like, I have this standing desk now in my office. Well, it's not a real standing desk, I just stacked a bunch of old phone books under the legs of my regular desk. Do you remember phone books? The Yellow Pages? It's wild that they used to just drop those massive books on everyone's doorstep every year. I used to use them all the time when I first started out as an investigator. You could find anyone in there. Now everything is online, which makes it easier, but also harder because people know how to scrub their data. I had to take this whole weekend seminar on digital footprint recovery last month. It was in this awful hotel conference room with no windows, and the air conditioning was blasting the entire time. I swear it was like 50 degrees in there. I had to wear my winter coat indoors. The instructor was this guy who used to work for the NSA, supposedly, but he spent half the time talking about his cryptocurrency investments. He kept telling us we needed to buy into this new coin, I can't even remember the name of it. Something with a dog logo, I think. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if I have trouble finishing tasks. I just know I can't sit in a regular chair to do them.\n\nDoctor: When you start working on a case or another project, do you typically finish it, or do you find yourself abandoning it partway through and moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: Finish them? Um... I'm really not sure. I guess I don't really pay attention to whether I abandon them or not. I mean, my cases usually get done, but I don't know if I'm leaving other things half-finished. \n\nIt's kind of like my house, honestly. I started painting my living room last spring. Just one wall, an accent wall, you know? I picked out this really nice sage green color. The guy at the hardware store said it was their most popular shade. He was a nice guy, older, wore those thick glasses that make your eyes look huge. He told me he'd been working there for thirty years, back when it was an independent shop before the big chain bought them out. He said the original owner used to bring in donuts every Friday from this bakery down the street. That bakery is actually still there! I go there sometimes when I'm on a stakeout early in the morning. They have these amazing bear claws, the kind with real almond paste inside, not that artificial flavored stuff. You can always tell the difference. I tried making my own pastries once during lockdown. Bought all the yeast and flour, even ordered a special pastry blender online. It arrived in this huge box with way too much bubble wrap. My cat, Barnaby—he's a rescue, part Maine Coon, I think—he went absolutely crazy for the bubble wrap. He was popping it for days. I kept finding little shreds of plastic all over the carpet. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't really know if I abandon projects. I just kind of do things as they come up, I guess?\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, can you give me an example of a project or task you started but never actually finished?\n\nPatient: An example? Um... I'm drawing a blank, honestly. I don't really keep a list of things like that, so nothing is really coming to mind. My brain just doesn't categorize things that way. It's more like a filing cabinet where someone dropped all the folders on the floor. \n\nSpeaking of folders, I actually had to buy all new manila folders for my office last week. The office supply store downtown was having this massive clearance sale because they're closing. It's really sad, actually. The owner, Mr. Henderson, has been running that place since the nineties. He said the rent just got too high for a small business. It's happening all over that block, which is a shame. The little Italian place next door to him closed last year, too. They had the absolute best garlic knots. They used real butter and fresh parsley, not that dried stuff in the little plastic shaker. I actually tried growing my own parsley once on my balcony. Well, I say balcony, but it's really just a tiny fire escape. The pigeons out there are completely fearless. One of them actually flew right into my apartment when I left the sliding door open this summer. It took me two hours to chase it back outside with a broom. Barnaby—my cat—was absolutely no help at all. He just sat on top of the refrigerator watching me run around like a maniac. \n\nBut yeah, an example of a project? I really don't know. I just can't think of one off the top of my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just kind of do things as they come up, I guess?\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, can you give me an example of a project or task you started but never actually finished?\n\nPatient: An example? Um... I'm drawing a blank, honestly. I don't really keep a list of things like that, so nothing is really coming to mind. My brain just doesn't categorize things that way. It's more like a filing cabinet where someone dropped all the folders on the floor. \n\nSpeaking of folders, I actually had to buy all new manila folders for my office last week. The office supply store downtown was having this massive clearance sale because they're closing. It's really sad, actually. The owner, Mr. Henderson, has been running that place since the nineties. He said the rent just got too high for a small business. It's happening all over that block, which is a shame. The little Italian place next door to him closed last year, too. They had the absolute best garlic knots. They used real butter and fresh parsley, not that dried stuff in the little plastic shaker. I actually tried growing my own parsley once on my balcony. Well, I say balcony, but it's really just a tiny fire escape. The pigeons out there are completely fearless. One of them actually flew right into my apartment when I left the sliding door open this summer. It took me two hours to chase it back outside with a broom. Barnaby—my cat—was absolutely no help at all. He just sat on top of the refrigerator watching me run around like a maniac. \n\nBut yeah, an example of a project? I really don't know. I just can't think of one off the top of my head.\n\n[Last Question]\nIn your work as an investigator, do you find it hard to organize your cases and keep track of deadlines, or do you manage that part pretty well even though you have to move around?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Organize my cases? Um... I don't really know if I have trouble with that. I mean, I guess I have a system, sort of? I don't really think about whether it's hard to keep track of deadlines or not, I just kind of do what needs to be done when it needs to be done. 

I do have these really great expanding file folders, though. They're color-coded. I got them at that same office supply store I was talking about. I bought like, ten of them in this bright neon pink color. They were on clearance, probably because nobody else wanted neon pink. But I actually love pink. I had a pink bicycle when I was a kid. It had those sparkly streamers on the handlebars and a white basket on the front. I used to put my stuffed animals in the basket and ride them around the neighborhood. My favorite was this little stuffed raccoon named Bandit. I won him at the county fair playing that game where you have to shoot the water into the clown's mouth to make the balloon pop. My dad spent like twenty dollars just so I could win that raccoon. He was always doing stuff like that. We used to go to the fair every single summer. They had these incredible funnel cakes with powdered sugar that would get all over your clothes. You could never eat one without looking like you'd been in a snowstorm afterward. 

So, yeah, organizing? I'm not really sure if it's hard for me or not. I just use my pink folders and hope for the best, I guess.

Yeah, um, what brings me in is that I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to stay put. I'm always jumping up to refill a glass or look at a bookshelf or whatever, and they're starting to give me these looks.\n\nDoctor: When you're in these situations where you can't stay still, do you also find it hard to concentrate or focus on what's being said, or do you find your mind wandering?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? Has this been going on since you were younger, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, how long? Well, I mean, I've always been an active person, but this specific... like, this really intense need to move? It's been noticeably worse for about eight months. Yeah, right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. \n\nI loved that place. It was a third-floor walk-up in Lincoln Park, right by the conservatory. The light in the living room was just incredible in the afternoons. I had this huge monstera plant that absolutely thrived there. It grew so big I actually had to repot it three times in the five years I lived there. Repotting is such a messy job, though. I always ended up with soil all over the hardwood floors, and then I'd have to sweep it up, but you can never get all the grit, you know? It always feels like you're walking on sandpaper for days afterward. I tried putting down newspaper once, but the water leaked through and stained the wood, which was a whole other disaster. I ended up having to buy this special rug just to cover the spot before the landlord saw it. It was a really nice rug, actually, sort of a Persian style, deep reds and blues. I found it at this antique market on a Sunday morning. The guy wanted way too much for it, but I managed to haggle him down. I'm pretty good at haggling, actually. It's a useful skill in my line of work. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, the moving thing. It's really just been the last eight months or so that it's gotten this bad.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been an active person. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, just investigating every corner instead of sitting at my desk. \n\nWhich is kind of funny, considering what I do for a living now. But back then, I just really hated those little wooden desks with the chairs attached. They were so restrictive. And they always had those weird grooves at the top for pencils, but my pencils would always roll right off anyway. I used to use those Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the green metal band near the eraser. They were the best. The lead didn't break every five seconds like the cheap ones did. I actually still use them for my fieldwork notes. There's just something about writing in pencil that feels more... permanent, ironically? Like, ink can smear if it rains while you're on a stakeout, but pencil holds up. I got caught in a downpour once outside a warehouse in Gary, Indiana, and my whole notebook was soaked, but I could still read every single word I'd written. Gary's a tough town, though. The wind off Lake Michigan just cuts right through you in the winter. You really have to layer up if you're going to be sitting in a car for hours. No, no one's ever formally evaluated me or anything. Definitely no medication. I mean, my mom just thought I had a lot of energy, you know? She never took me to a doctor for it. \n\nHonestly, I've always been a little wary of doctors anyway. Not you, of course, but just... the whole medical establishment. It started when I broke my arm in high school. I was climbing this massive oak tree in our backyard—I used to love climbing trees, you could see the whole neighborhood from up there. Anyway, a branch snapped, and I fell right onto my left arm. The doctor at the ER set the bone wrong, and they had to re-break it two weeks later to fix it. It was excruciating. Ever since then, I just try to avoid hospitals if I can help it. I don't even like the smell of them. That weird, sterile, rubbing-alcohol smell? It instantly puts me on edge. It reminds me of this one case I worked a few years ago involving medical supply fraud. I had to spend three weeks doing surveillance on this warehouse that distributed rubbing alcohol and bandages. The smell seeped into my clothes, my car, everything. I had to get my car detailed twice just to get the scent out of the upholstery. The guy who did the detailing was great, though. His name was Hector, and he used this citrus spray that smelled amazing. I actually asked him what brand it was, but he said it was a family secret recipe. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no official diagnosis or pills or anything like that. Just me and my energy.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home alone, just relaxing or doing something you enjoy—like working on a case or reading—do you still feel that same need to get up and move around, or does it mostly happen when you're with other people or in situations where you're supposed to stay still?\n\nPatient: When I'm at home alone? Yeah, it's... it's still there. Even with things I actually enjoy doing. Like, I used to love quiet things to just unwind. Yoga, for instance. Or going to my book club. But lately, those quiet things that used to help me relax? We were reading this mystery novel—which, honestly, the detective work in it was laughable. The protagonist didn't even check the suspect's alibi against the cell tower data. I mean, that's day-one stuff! If I ran my investigations like that, I'd have lost my license years ago. You always check the digital footprint. People lie, but their phones don't. I had this one case where a guy swore he was in Milwaukee for a conference, but his phone pinged off a tower three blocks from his wife's sister's house in Evanston. Turns out he was hiding his car in her garage. It was a classic setup. The garage was one of those old detached ones, with the peeling white paint and the heavy wooden doors that scrape the concrete when you open them. I had to sit in my car across the alley for six hours waiting for him to come out. I drank so much coffee that day, I thought my heart was going to beat right out of my chest. It was from this little diner on the corner, they made it way too strong. \n\nBut yeah, even when I'm trying to relax and read or do yoga, I just... I can't stay still anymore. I'm always shifting or finding a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on your investigation cases or other tasks that require sustained focus, do you find it hard to stick with them from start to finish, or do you generally complete what you start even though you need to move around while doing it?\n\nPatient: Focusing on cases? Um... I don't really know if I have trouble sticking with them. I mean, I guess I get them done eventually? I'm not really sure if it's hard for me to finish things or if I just do them my own way. \n\nI do have to move around a lot while I'm working, though. Like, I have this standing desk now in my office. Well, it's not a real standing desk, I just stacked a bunch of old phone books under the legs of my regular desk. Do you remember phone books? The Yellow Pages? It's wild that they used to just drop those massive books on everyone's doorstep every year. I used to use them all the time when I first started out as an investigator. You could find anyone in there. Now everything is online, which makes it easier, but also harder because people know how to scrub their data. I had to take this whole weekend seminar on digital footprint recovery last month. It was in this awful hotel conference room with no windows, and the air conditioning was blasting the entire time. I swear it was like 50 degrees in there. I had to wear my winter coat indoors. The instructor was this guy who used to work for the NSA, supposedly, but he spent half the time talking about his cryptocurrency investments. He kept telling us we needed to buy into this new coin, I can't even remember the name of it. Something with a dog logo, I think. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if I have trouble finishing tasks. I just know I can't sit in a regular chair to do them.\n\nDoctor: When you start working on a case or another project, do you typically finish it, or do you find yourself abandoning it partway through and moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: Finish them? Um... I'm really not sure. I guess I don't really pay attention to whether I abandon them or not. I mean, my cases usually get done, but I don't know if I'm leaving other things half-finished. \n\nIt's kind of like my house, honestly. I started painting my living room last spring. Just one wall, an accent wall, you know? I picked out this really nice sage green color. The guy at the hardware store said it was their most popular shade. He was a nice guy, older, wore those thick glasses that make your eyes look huge. He told me he'd been working there for thirty years, back when it was an independent shop before the big chain bought them out. He said the original owner used to bring in donuts every Friday from this bakery down the street. That bakery is actually still there! I go there sometimes when I'm on a stakeout early in the morning. They have these amazing bear claws, the kind with real almond paste inside, not that artificial flavored stuff. You can always tell the difference. I tried making my own pastries once during lockdown. Bought all the yeast and flour, even ordered a special pastry blender online. It arrived in this huge box with way too much bubble wrap. My cat, Barnaby—he's a rescue, part Maine Coon, I think—he went absolutely crazy for the bubble wrap. He was popping it for days. I kept finding little shreds of plastic all over the carpet. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't really know if I abandon projects. I just kind of do things as they come up, I guess?\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, can you give me an example of a project or task you started but never actually finished?\n\nPatient: An example? Barnaby—my cat—was absolutely no help at all. He just sat on top of the refrigerator watching me run around like a maniac. \n\nBut yeah, an example of a project? I really don't know. Um... I don't really know if I have trouble with that. I mean, I guess I have a system, sort of? I don't really think about whether it's hard to keep track of deadlines or not, I just kind of do what needs to be done when it needs to be done. \n\nI do have these really great expanding file folders, though. He did this hilarious high-stepping walk like a dressage horse for about two minutes before he just lay down and refused to move until I took them off. I ended up giving them to my neighbor who has a poodle. Poodles seem more like the bootie-wearing type, anyway. \n\nBut yeah, I guess that's what it feels like. Just this physical need to burn off energy, not nervousness or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this need to move around and this physical restlessness? Has this been going on since you were younger, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, how long? Well, I mean, I've always been an active person, but this specific... like, this really intense need to move? It's been noticeably worse for about eight months. Yeah, right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. \n\nI loved that place. It was a third-floor walk-up in Lincoln Park, right by the conservatory. The light in the living room was just incredible in the afternoons. I had this huge monstera plant that absolutely thrived there. It grew so big I actually had to repot it three times in the five years I lived there. Repotting is such a messy job, though. I always ended up with soil all over the hardwood floors, and then I'd have to sweep it up, but you can never get all the grit, you know? It always feels like you're walking on sandpaper for days afterward. I tried putting down newspaper once, but the water leaked through and stained the wood, which was a whole other disaster. I ended up having to buy this special rug just to cover the spot before the landlord saw it. It was a really nice rug, actually, sort of a Persian style, deep reds and blues. I found it at this antique market on a Sunday morning. The guy wanted way too much for it, but I managed to haggle him down. I'm pretty good at haggling, actually. It's a useful skill in my line of work. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, the moving thing. It's really just been the last eight months or so that it's gotten this bad.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been an active person. When you were a child or teenager, did teachers or family ever say you were hyperactive, or did you have trouble sitting still in school or at the dinner table—even back then?\n\nPatient: School? Oh, yeah. I mean, my grade-school report cards always said I was bright, but they always had these notes about how I couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, just investigating every corner instead of sitting at my desk. \n\nWhich is kind of funny, considering what I do for a living now. But back then, I just really hated those little wooden desks with the chairs attached. They were so restrictive. And they always had those weird grooves at the top for pencils, but my pencils would always roll right off anyway. I used to use those Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the green metal band near the eraser. They were the best. The lead didn't break every five seconds like the cheap ones did. I actually still use them for my fieldwork notes. There's just something about writing in pencil that feels more... permanent, ironically? Like, ink can smear if it rains while you're on a stakeout, but pencil holds up. I got caught in a downpour once outside a warehouse in Gary, Indiana, and my whole notebook was soaked, but I could still read every single word I'd written. Gary's a tough town, though. The wind off Lake Michigan just cuts right through you in the winter. You really have to layer up if you're going to be sitting in a car for hours. Thermal socks are an absolute must. \n\nBut yeah, my mom always said I couldn't sit still when I was a kid. It's just who I am, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been formally diagnosed with ADHD or evaluated for it by a doctor? And if so, were you prescribed any medication for it?\n\nPatient: Diagnosed? No, no one's ever formally evaluated me or anything. Definitely no medication. I mean, my mom just thought I had a lot of energy, you know? She never took me to a doctor for it. \n\nHonestly, I've always been a little wary of doctors anyway. Not you, of course, but just... the whole medical establishment. It started when I broke my arm in high school. I was climbing this massive oak tree in our backyard—I used to love climbing trees, you could see the whole neighborhood from up there. Anyway, a branch snapped, and I fell right onto my left arm. The doctor at the ER set the bone wrong, and they had to re-break it two weeks later to fix it. It was excruciating. Ever since then, I just try to avoid hospitals if I can help it. I don't even like the smell of them. That weird, sterile, rubbing-alcohol smell? It instantly puts me on edge. It reminds me of this one case I worked a few years ago involving medical supply fraud. I had to spend three weeks doing surveillance on this warehouse that distributed rubbing alcohol and bandages. The smell seeped into my clothes, my car, everything. I had to get my car detailed twice just to get the scent out of the upholstery. The guy who did the detailing was great, though. His name was Hector, and he used this citrus spray that smelled amazing. I actually asked him what brand it was, but he said it was a family secret recipe. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no official diagnosis or pills or anything like that. Just me and my energy.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home alone, just relaxing or doing something you enjoy—like working on a case or reading—do you still feel that same need to get up and move around, or does it mostly happen when you're with other people or in situations where you're supposed to stay still?\n\nPatient: When I'm at home alone? Yeah, it's... it's still there. Even with things I actually enjoy doing. Like, I used to love quiet things to just unwind. Yoga, for instance. Or going to my book club. But lately, those quiet things that used to help me relax? They just turn into me whispering comments to the person next to me, or rustling my papers, or finding some excuse to get up and move around instead of just being still. \n\nI was actually at book club last Tuesday. We were reading this mystery novel—which, honestly, the detective work in it was laughable. The protagonist didn't even check the suspect's alibi against the cell tower data. I mean, that's day-one stuff! If I ran my investigations like that, I'd have lost my license years ago. You always check the digital footprint. People lie, but their phones don't. I had this one case where a guy swore he was in Milwaukee for a conference, but his phone pinged off a tower three blocks from his wife's sister's house in Evanston. Turns out he was hiding his car in her garage. It was a classic setup. The garage was one of those old detached ones, with the peeling white paint and the heavy wooden doors that scrape the concrete when you open them. I had to sit in my car across the alley for six hours waiting for him to come out. I drank so much coffee that day, I thought my heart was going to beat right out of my chest. It was from this little diner on the corner, they made it way too strong. \n\nBut yeah, even when I'm trying to relax and read or do yoga, I just... I can't stay still anymore. I'm always shifting or finding a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on your investigation cases or other tasks that require sustained focus, do you find it hard to stick with them from start to finish, or do you generally complete what you start even though you need to move around while doing it?\n\nPatient: Focusing on cases? Um... I don't really know if I have trouble sticking with them. I mean, I guess I get them done eventually? I'm not really sure if it's hard for me to finish things or if I just do them my own way. \n\nI do have to move around a lot while I'm working, though. Like, I have this standing desk now in my office. Well, it's not a real standing desk, I just stacked a bunch of old phone books under the legs of my regular desk. Do you remember phone books? The Yellow Pages? It's wild that they used to just drop those massive books on everyone's doorstep every year. I used to use them all the time when I first started out as an investigator. You could find anyone in there. Now everything is online, which makes it easier, but also harder because people know how to scrub their data. I had to take this whole weekend seminar on digital footprint recovery last month. It was in this awful hotel conference room with no windows, and the air conditioning was blasting the entire time. I swear it was like 50 degrees in there. I had to wear my winter coat indoors. The instructor was this guy who used to work for the NSA, supposedly, but he spent half the time talking about his cryptocurrency investments. He kept telling us we needed to buy into this new coin, I can't even remember the name of it. Something with a dog logo, I think. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if I have trouble finishing tasks. I just know I can't sit in a regular chair to do them.\n\nDoctor: When you start working on a case or another project, do you typically finish it, or do you find yourself abandoning it partway through and moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: Finish them? Um... I'm really not sure. I guess I don't really pay attention to whether I abandon them or not. I mean, my cases usually get done, but I don't know if I'm leaving other things half-finished. \n\nIt's kind of like my house, honestly. I started painting my living room last spring. Just one wall, an accent wall, you know? I picked out this really nice sage green color. The guy at the hardware store said it was their most popular shade. He was a nice guy, older, wore those thick glasses that make your eyes look huge. He told me he'd been working there for thirty years, back when it was an independent shop before the big chain bought them out. He said the original owner used to bring in donuts every Friday from this bakery down the street. That bakery is actually still there! I go there sometimes when I'm on a stakeout early in the morning. They have these amazing bear claws, the kind with real almond paste inside, not that artificial flavored stuff. You can always tell the difference. I tried making my own pastries once during lockdown. Bought all the yeast and flour, even ordered a special pastry blender online. It arrived in this huge box with way too much bubble wrap. My cat, Barnaby—he's a rescue, part Maine Coon, I think—he went absolutely crazy for the bubble wrap. He was popping it for days. I kept finding little shreds of plastic all over the carpet. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't really know if I abandon projects. I just kind of do things as they come up, I guess?\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, can you give me an example of a project or task you started but never actually finished?\n\nPatient: An example? Um... I'm drawing a blank, honestly. I don't really keep a list of things like that, so nothing is really coming to mind. My brain just doesn't categorize things that way. It's more like a filing cabinet where someone dropped all the folders on the floor. \n\nSpeaking of folders, I actually had to buy all new manila folders for my office last week. The office supply store downtown was having this massive clearance sale because they're closing. It's really sad, actually. The owner, Mr. Henderson, has been running that place since the nineties. He said the rent just got too high for a small business. It's happening all over that block, which is a shame. The little Italian place next door to him closed last year, too. They had the absolute best garlic knots. They used real butter and fresh parsley, not that dried stuff in the little plastic shaker. I actually tried growing my own parsley once on my balcony. Well, I say balcony, but it's really just a tiny fire escape. The pigeons out there are completely fearless. One of them actually flew right into my apartment when I left the sliding door open this summer. It took me two hours to chase it back outside with a broom. Barnaby—my cat—was absolutely no help at all. He just sat on top of the refrigerator watching me run around like a maniac. \n\nBut yeah, an example of a project? I really don't know. I just can't think of one off the top of my head.\n\nDoctor: In your work as an investigator, do you find it hard to organize your cases and keep track of deadlines, or do you manage that part pretty well even though you have to move around?\n\nPatient: Organize my cases? Um... I don't really know if I have trouble with that. I mean, I guess I have a system, sort of? I don't really think about whether it's hard to keep track of deadlines or not, I just kind of do what needs to be done when it needs to be done. \n\nI do have these really great expanding file folders, though. They're color-coded. I got them at that same office supply store I was talking about. I bought like, ten of them in this bright neon pink color. They were on clearance, probably because nobody else wanted neon pink. But I actually love pink. I had a pink bicycle when I was a kid. It had those sparkly streamers on the handlebars and a white basket on the front. I used to put my stuffed animals in the basket and ride them around the neighborhood. My favorite was this little stuffed raccoon named Bandit. I won him at the county fair playing that game where you have to shoot the water into the clown's mouth to make the balloon pop. My dad spent like twenty dollars just so I could win that raccoon. He was always doing stuff like that. We used to go to the fair every single summer. They had these incredible funnel cakes with powdered sugar that would get all over your clothes. You could never eat one without looking like you'd been in a snowstorm afterward. \n\nSo, yeah, organizing? I'm not really sure if it's hard for me or not. I just use my pink folders and hope for the best, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Deciding which case to work on first? Um... I'm not really sure if I find that hard. I don't really think about it like that, I just kind of grab whatever folder is closest to me, I guess? 

Like right now, the one sitting on top of my desk is this insurance fraud thing. It's about this guy who says he slipped and hurt his back at a bowling alley. Which is funny, because I actually used to go to that exact same bowling alley all the time in my twenties. They used to do this midnight rock-n-bowl thing on Fridays. They'd turn off all the regular lights and turn on these huge blacklights, and they had a DJ who would play mostly eighties music. I actually dropped a bowling ball on my foot there once. It was one of those really heavy, sparkly blue ones. My big toenail turned completely black and eventually just fell right off. It was so gross. I had to wear open-toed shoes for like six weeks, which was a total nightmare because I was just starting out doing a lot of surveillance jobs back then, and you really can't go tramping through the brush looking into people's backyards wearing strappy sandals. You get mosquito bites all over your ankles. I actually bought this natural bug spray made with citronella and eucalyptus, but it smelled so strong I think it gave away my position a couple of times. It smelled exactly like those big outdoor candles you burn on the patio. I love that smell, actually, it reminds me of camping. 

But anyway, what were you asking? Oh, keeping track of cases? Yeah, I don't really know if I struggle with that. I just sort of work on whatever is in front of me until I have to do something else.

Yeah, right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. \n\nI loved that place. It was a third-floor walk-up in Lincoln Park, right by the conservatory. I used to use those Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the green metal band near the eraser. They were the best. The lead didn't break every five seconds like the cheap ones did. Anyway, a branch snapped, and I fell right onto my left arm. The doctor at the ER set the bone wrong, and they had to re-break it two weeks later to fix it. It was excruciating. Ever since then, I just try to avoid hospitals if I can help it. I don't even like the smell of them. That weird, sterile, rubbing-alcohol smell? It instantly puts me on edge. It reminds me of this one case I worked a few years ago involving medical supply fraud. I had to spend three weeks doing surveillance on this warehouse that distributed rubbing alcohol and bandages. You could find anyone in there. Now everything is online, which makes it easier, but also harder because people know how to scrub their data. Um... I'm really not sure. I guess I don't really pay attention to whether I abandon them or not. No, it's not anxiety. I'm not really a nervous person. It's more like... like a motor running inside me, I guess? Like this pressure that just builds up and I have to move to let it out. It's totally physical. \n\nIt kind of reminds me of my golden retriever, Buster. He's such a good boy, but if I don't take him for a long walk in the morning, he just vibrates with energy all day. He'll pace around the living room, bring me every toy he owns, just whining until we go out. We usually walk down by the river. There's this great trail there that used to be an old railway line. They paved it over a few years ago, which is nice for walking, but honestly, I kind of miss the dirt path. It felt more natural, you know? Plus, the pavement gets so hot in the summer, I always worry about Buster's paws. I actually bought him these little dog booties once to protect his feet, but he hated them so much. He did this hilarious high-stepping walk like a dressage horse for about two minutes before he just lay down and refused to move until I took them off. I ended up giving them to my neighbor who has a poodle. Poodles seem more like the bootie-wearing type, anyway. \n\nBut yeah, I guess that's what it feels like. Just this physical need to burn off energy, not nervousness or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this need to move around and this physical restlessness? Has this been going on since you were younger, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, how long? Well, I mean, I've always been an active person, but this specific... like, this really intense need to move? It's been noticeably worse for about eight months. Yeah, right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. \n\nI loved that place. It was a third-floor walk-up in Lincoln Park, right by the conservatory. The light in the living room was just incredible in the afternoons. I had this huge monstera plant that absolutely thrived there. It grew so big I actually had to repot it three times in the five years I lived there. Repotting is such a messy job, though. I always ended up with soil all over the hardwood floors, and then I'd have to sweep it up, but you can never get all the grit, you know? It always feels like you're walking on sandpaper for days afterward. I tried putting down newspaper once, but the water leaked through and stained the wood, which was a whole other disaster. I ended up having to buy this special rug just to cover the spot before the landlord saw it. It was a really nice rug, actually, sort of a Persian style, deep reds and blues. I found it at this antique market on a Sunday morning. The guy wanted way too much for it, but I managed to haggle him down. I'm pretty good at haggling, actually. It's a useful skill in my line of work. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, the moving thing. It's really just been the last eight months or so that it's gotten this bad.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've always been an active person. When you were a child or teenager, did teachers or family ever say you were hyperactive, or did you have trouble sitting still in school or at the dinner table—even back then?\n\nPatient: School? Oh, yeah. I mean, my grade-school report cards always said I was bright, but they always had these notes about how I couldn't stay in my seat or wait my turn. My mother actually still jokes about it—she says I treated every classroom like a crime scene I had to pace through, just investigating every corner instead of sitting at my desk. \n\nWhich is kind of funny, considering what I do for a living now. But back then, I just really hated those little wooden desks with the chairs attached. They were so restrictive. And they always had those weird grooves at the top for pencils, but my pencils would always roll right off anyway. I used to use those Ticonderoga pencils, the yellow ones with the green metal band near the eraser. They were the best. The lead didn't break every five seconds like the cheap ones did. I actually still use them for my fieldwork notes. There's just something about writing in pencil that feels more... permanent, ironically? Like, ink can smear if it rains while you're on a stakeout, but pencil holds up. I got caught in a downpour once outside a warehouse in Gary, Indiana, and my whole notebook was soaked, but I could still read every single word I'd written. Gary's a tough town, though. The wind off Lake Michigan just cuts right through you in the winter. You really have to layer up if you're going to be sitting in a car for hours. Thermal socks are an absolute must. \n\nBut yeah, my mom always said I couldn't sit still when I was a kid. It's just who I am, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been formally diagnosed with ADHD or evaluated for it by a doctor? And if so, were you prescribed any medication for it?\n\nPatient: Diagnosed? No, no one's ever formally evaluated me or anything. Definitely no medication. I mean, my mom just thought I had a lot of energy, you know? She never took me to a doctor for it. \n\nHonestly, I've always been a little wary of doctors anyway. Not you, of course, but just... the whole medical establishment. It started when I broke my arm in high school. I was climbing this massive oak tree in our backyard—I used to love climbing trees, you could see the whole neighborhood from up there. Anyway, a branch snapped, and I fell right onto my left arm. The doctor at the ER set the bone wrong, and they had to re-break it two weeks later to fix it. It was excruciating. Ever since then, I just try to avoid hospitals if I can help it. I don't even like the smell of them. That weird, sterile, rubbing-alcohol smell? It instantly puts me on edge. It reminds me of this one case I worked a few years ago involving medical supply fraud. I had to spend three weeks doing surveillance on this warehouse that distributed rubbing alcohol and bandages. The smell seeped into my clothes, my car, everything. I had to get my car detailed twice just to get the scent out of the upholstery. The guy who did the detailing was great, though. His name was Hector, and he used this citrus spray that smelled amazing. I actually asked him what brand it was, but he said it was a family secret recipe. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, no official diagnosis or pills or anything like that. Just me and my energy.\n\nDoctor: When you're at home alone, just relaxing or doing something you enjoy—like working on a case or reading—do you still feel that same need to get up and move around, or does it mostly happen when you're with other people or in situations where you're supposed to stay still?\n\nPatient: When I'm at home alone? Yeah, it's... it's still there. Even with things I actually enjoy doing. Like, I used to love quiet things to just unwind. Yoga, for instance. Or going to my book club. But lately, those quiet things that used to help me relax? They just turn into me whispering comments to the person next to me, or rustling my papers, or finding some excuse to get up and move around instead of just being still. \n\nI was actually at book club last Tuesday. We were reading this mystery novel—which, honestly, the detective work in it was laughable. The protagonist didn't even check the suspect's alibi against the cell tower data. I mean, that's day-one stuff! If I ran my investigations like that, I'd have lost my license years ago. You always check the digital footprint. People lie, but their phones don't. I had this one case where a guy swore he was in Milwaukee for a conference, but his phone pinged off a tower three blocks from his wife's sister's house in Evanston. Turns out he was hiding his car in her garage. It was a classic setup. The garage was one of those old detached ones, with the peeling white paint and the heavy wooden doors that scrape the concrete when you open them. I had to sit in my car across the alley for six hours waiting for him to come out. I drank so much coffee that day, I thought my heart was going to beat right out of my chest. It was from this little diner on the corner, they made it way too strong. \n\nBut yeah, even when I'm trying to relax and read or do yoga, I just... I can't stay still anymore. I'm always shifting or finding a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on your investigation cases or other tasks that require sustained focus, do you find it hard to stick with them from start to finish, or do you generally complete what you start even though you need to move around while doing it?\n\nPatient: Focusing on cases? Um... I don't really know if I have trouble sticking with them. I mean, I guess I get them done eventually? I'm not really sure if it's hard for me to finish things or if I just do them my own way. \n\nI do have to move around a lot while I'm working, though. Like, I have this standing desk now in my office. Well, it's not a real standing desk, I just stacked a bunch of old phone books under the legs of my regular desk. Do you remember phone books? The Yellow Pages? It's wild that they used to just drop those massive books on everyone's doorstep every year. I used to use them all the time when I first started out as an investigator. You could find anyone in there. Now everything is online, which makes it easier, but also harder because people know how to scrub their data. I had to take this whole weekend seminar on digital footprint recovery last month. It was in this awful hotel conference room with no windows, and the air conditioning was blasting the entire time. I swear it was like 50 degrees in there. I had to wear my winter coat indoors. The instructor was this guy who used to work for the NSA, supposedly, but he spent half the time talking about his cryptocurrency investments. He kept telling us we needed to buy into this new coin, I can't even remember the name of it. Something with a dog logo, I think. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if I have trouble finishing tasks. I just know I can't sit in a regular chair to do them.\n\nDoctor: When you start working on a case or another project, do you typically finish it, or do you find yourself abandoning it partway through and moving on to something else?\n\nPatient: Finish them? Um... I'm really not sure. I guess I don't really pay attention to whether I abandon them or not. I mean, my cases usually get done, but I don't know if I'm leaving other things half-finished. \n\nIt's kind of like my house, honestly. I started painting my living room last spring. Just one wall, an accent wall, you know? I picked out this really nice sage green color. The guy at the hardware store said it was their most popular shade. He was a nice guy, older, wore those thick glasses that make your eyes look huge. He told me he'd been working there for thirty years, back when it was an independent shop before the big chain bought them out. He said the original owner used to bring in donuts every Friday from this bakery down the street. That bakery is actually still there! I go there sometimes when I'm on a stakeout early in the morning. They have these amazing bear claws, the kind with real almond paste inside, not that artificial flavored stuff. You can always tell the difference. I tried making my own pastries once during lockdown. Bought all the yeast and flour, even ordered a special pastry blender online. It arrived in this huge box with way too much bubble wrap. My cat, Barnaby—he's a rescue, part Maine Coon, I think—he went absolutely crazy for the bubble wrap. He was popping it for days. I kept finding little shreds of plastic all over the carpet. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, I don't really know if I abandon projects. I just kind of do things as they come up, I guess?\n\nDoctor: When you think back over the past few months, can you give me an example of a project or task you started but never actually finished?\n\nPatient: An example? Um... I'm drawing a blank, honestly. I don't really keep a list of things like that, so nothing is really coming to mind. My brain just doesn't categorize things that way. It's more like a filing cabinet where someone dropped all the folders on the floor. \n\nSpeaking of folders, I actually had to buy all new manila folders for my office last week. The office supply store downtown was having this massive clearance sale because they're closing. It's really sad, actually. The owner, Mr. Henderson, has been running that place since the nineties. He said the rent just got too high for a small business. It's happening all over that block, which is a shame. The little Italian place next door to him closed last year, too. They had the absolute best garlic knots. They used real butter and fresh parsley, not that dried stuff in the little plastic shaker. I actually tried growing my own parsley once on my balcony. Well, I say balcony, but it's really just a tiny fire escape. The pigeons out there are completely fearless. One of them actually flew right into my apartment when I left the sliding door open this summer. It took me two hours to chase it back outside with a broom. Barnaby—my cat—was absolutely no help at all. He just sat on top of the refrigerator watching me run around like a maniac. \n\nBut yeah, an example of a project? I really don't know. I just can't think of one off the top of my head.\n\nDoctor: In your work as an investigator, do you find it hard to organize your cases and keep track of deadlines, or do you manage that part pretty well even though you have to move around?\n\nPatient: Organize my cases? Um... I don't really know if I have trouble with that. I mean, I guess I have a system, sort of? I don't really think about whether it's hard to keep track of deadlines or not, I just kind of do what needs to be done when it needs to be done. \n\nI do have these really great expanding file folders, though. They're color-coded. I got them at that same office supply store I was talking about. I bought like, ten of them in this bright neon pink color. They were on clearance, probably because nobody else wanted neon pink. But I actually love pink. I had a pink bicycle when I was a kid. It had those sparkly streamers on the handlebars and a white basket on the front. I used to put my stuffed animals in the basket and ride them around the neighborhood. My favorite was this little stuffed raccoon named Bandit. I won him at the county fair playing that game where you have to shoot the water into the clown's mouth to make the balloon pop. My dad spent like twenty dollars just so I could win that raccoon. He was always doing stuff like that. We used to go to the fair every single summer. They had these incredible funnel cakes with powdered sugar that would get all over your clothes. You could never eat one without looking like you'd been in a snowstorm afterward. \n\nSo, yeah, organizing? I'm not really sure if it's hard for me or not. I just use my pink folders and hope for the best, I guess.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on multiple investigation cases at the same time, do you find it hard to decide which one to work on first, or do you struggle to keep track of where you are with each case?\n\nPatient: Deciding which case to work on first? Um... I'm not really sure if I find that hard. I don't really think about it like that, I just kind of grab whatever folder is closest to me, I guess? \n\nLike right now, the one sitting on top of my desk is this insurance fraud thing. It's about this guy who says he slipped and hurt his back at a bowling alley. Which is funny, because I actually used to go to that exact same bowling alley all the time in my twenties. They used to do this midnight rock-n-bowl thing on Fridays. They'd turn off all the regular lights and turn on these huge blacklights, and they had a DJ who would play mostly eighties music. I actually dropped a bowling ball on my foot there once. It was one of those really heavy, sparkly blue ones. My big toenail turned completely black and eventually just fell right off. It was so gross. I had to wear open-toed shoes for like six weeks, which was a total nightmare because I was just starting out doing a lot of surveillance jobs back then, and you really can't go tramping through the brush looking into people's backyards wearing strappy sandals. You get mosquito bites all over your ankles. I actually bought this natural bug spray made with citronella and eucalyptus, but it smelled so strong I think it gave away my position a couple of times. It smelled exactly like those big outdoor candles you burn on the patio. I love that smell, actually, it reminds me of camping. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, keeping track of cases? Yeah, I don't really know if I struggle with that. I just sort of work on whatever is in front of me until I have to do something else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It's about this guy who says he slipped and hurt his back at a bowling alley. Which is funny, because I actually used to go to that exact same bowling alley all the time in my twenties. They used to do this midnight rock-n-bowl thing on Fridays. They'd turn off all the regular lights and turn on these huge blacklights, and they had a DJ who would play mostly eighties music. I actually dropped a bowling ball on my foot there once. It was one of those really heavy, sparkly blue ones. My big toenail turned completely black and eventually just fell right off. It was so gross. I had to wear open-toed shoes for like six weeks, which was a total nightmare because I was just starting out doing a lot of surveillance jobs back then, and you really can't go tramping through the brush looking into people's backyards wearing strappy sandals. You get mosquito bites all over your ankles. I actually bought this natural bug spray made with citronella and eucalyptus, but it smelled so strong I think it gave away my position a couple of times. It smelled exactly like those big outdoor candles you burn on the patio. I love that smell, actually, it reminds me of camping. \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, keeping track of cases? Yeah, I don't really know if I struggle with that. I just sort of work on whatever is in front of me until I have to do something else.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past few months, have you had situations where you started working on something—like a case or a project—and then realized days or weeks later that you never actually finished it, even though you meant to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 49 / private_detective_or_investigator / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Starting something and realizing later I didn't finish it? Um... I don't think so? I mean, I can't really recall a specific time that happened recently. It's possible, I suppose, but nothing jumps out at me. 

I did find a coffee cup in the microwave yesterday that I must have put in there to reheat on, like, Tuesday. It was completely fuzzy on top. The mold was almost a pretty color, though, sort of a seafoam green. It reminded me of the paint color in my grandmother's kitchen. She had this huge, loud refrigerator from the seventies that was that exact same color. It used to hum so loudly you could hear it in the living room over the television. We used to watch those old game shows together, the ones with the really tacky carpets and the hosts with the skinny microphones. She loved the one where they had to guess the prices of groceries. She was actually amazing at it because she kept every single grocery receipt in this old shoebox. It was a Nike box, I think. Back when the swoosh was bright orange. I tried to start running a few years ago and bought these incredibly expensive running shoes, but they gave me terrible blisters on my heels. I ended up giving them to my sister, but her feet are a half size smaller than mine, so she had to wear two pairs of thick wool socks just to keep them from slipping off. 

So, yeah, starting something and not finishing it? I'm not really sure if I do that. I just don't really notice if I do.

He did this hilarious high-stepping walk like a dressage horse for about two minutes before he just lay down and refused to move until I took them off. I ended up giving them to my neighbor who has a poodle. Poodles seem more like the bootie-wearing type, anyway. \n\nBut yeah, I guess that's what it feels like. Just this physical need to burn off energy, not nervousness or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this need to move around and this physical restlessness? Has this been going on since you were younger, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, how long? Well, I mean, I've always been an active person, but this specific... like, this really intense need to move? It's been noticeably worse for about eight months. Yeah, right around the time I had to move out of my old apartment in Chicago. \n\nI loved that place. It was a third-floor walk-up in Lincoln Park, right by the conservatory. The light in the living room was just incredible in the afternoons. I had this huge monstera plant that absolutely thrived there. It grew so big I actually had to repot it three times in the five years I lived there. Repotting is such a messy job, though. I always ended up with soil all over the hardwood floors, and then I'd have to sweep it up, but you can never get all the grit, you know? It always feels like you're walking on sandpaper for days afterward. I tried putting down newspaper once, but the water leaked through and stained the wood, which was a whole other disaster. I ended up having to buy this special rug just to cover the spot before the landlord saw it. It was a really nice rug, actually, sort of a Persian style, deep reds and blues. I found it at this antique market on a Sunday morning. The guy wanted way too much for it, but I managed to haggle him down. I'm pretty good at haggling, actually. Anyway, a branch snapped, and I fell right onto my left arm. The doctor at the ER set the bone wrong, and they had to re-break it two weeks later to fix it. It was excruciating. I'm not really sure if it's hard for me to finish things or if I just do them my own way. \n\nI do have to move around a lot while I'm working, though. Like, I have this standing desk now in my office. Well, it's not a real standing desk, I just stacked a bunch of old phone books under the legs of my regular desk. Do you remember phone books? The Yellow Pages? It's wild that they used to just drop those massive books on everyone's doorstep every year. I used to use them all the time when I first started out as an investigator. You could find anyone in there. Now everything is online, which makes it easier, but also harder because people know how to scrub their data. I mean, my cases usually get done, but I don't know if I'm leaving other things half-finished. \n\nIt's kind of like my house, honestly. I started painting my living room last spring. He was a nice guy, older, wore those thick glasses that make your eyes look huge. He said the original owner used to bring in donuts every Friday from this bakery down the street. That bakery is actually still there! You can always tell the difference. I tried making my own pastries once during lockdown. Bought all the yeast and flour, even ordered a special pastry blender online. One of them actually flew right into my apartment when I left the sliding door open this summer. It took me two hours to chase it back outside with a broom.   "candidates": ["F90.9"],
